  Summary

  Fixed both issues:

  1. "Relative to Leg" missing" — not a bug. It only appears on Leg 2+ (needs an
  earlier leg to reference), verified live with a 2-leg session.
  2. Legwise only had "Roll: T-n", no separate entry/exit like Positional — this
  was a real gap, and fixing it surfaced a genuine engine bug along the way:
    - Added a new per-leg entry_trigger_days field
  (crates/iengine/src/strategy.rs), mirroring the existing exit-side
  rollover_trigger_days.
    - Rewrote the leg-wise engine loop so every cycle computes its own entry
  (Entry T-n before that contract's own expiry) and exit (Exit T-n)
  independently — exactly like Positional's Entry/Exit DTE, just scoped per-leg
  and repeated per contract, per your explicit answer ("same like positional
  entry... but legwise").
    - Along the way, found and fixed a real correctness bug this exposed:
  without a forward-progress check, the loop could keep trying every future
  listed expiry once it ran past the loaded data horizon, occasionally latching
  onto a later, unrelated contract's price data. Added a guard that stops the
  loop once a cycle makes no new progress in real trading days.
    - Updated/added engine tests (all 3 leg-wise/rel-to-leg tests updated for
  the new semantics), full workspace suite green (49/32/17/8 passing).
    - Live-verified against real NIFTY July 2026 data: Entry T-3/Exit T-0
  correctly produced 07-02→07-07, 07-09→07-14, 07-16→07-21, 07-23→07-28 —
  independent per-cycle windows with real gaps between them, tail cycle
  correctly range-clamped.
    - Wired the new field through the frontend (LegCard.jsx), optimizer
  (optimizer.js + ioptim), and fixed a CSS layout bug where the new "Exit" label
  split from its input across two lines.

  Everything is deployed live and screenshot-verified. Let me know if there's
  more UI you want swept.

● Removed the "÷ expiry count ÷ own lots" hint from the ref-leg picker —
  deployed. Here's how the formula actually works, with a concrete example:

  Formula: target premium = ref_leg's premium ÷ (how many of THIS leg's expiries
  fit inside ref_leg's cycle) ÷ this leg's lots

  The engine then picks whichever strike on this leg has premium closest to that
  target.

  Example — Leg 1 = Monthly CE (sell), Leg 2 = Weekly PE, refLeg = 1:

  - Entry day, Leg 1's Monthly contract premium resolves to ₹500.
  - A calendar month has 4 Weekly expiries → divisor = 4.
  - Leg 2 has 1 lot.
  - Target = 500 ÷ 4 ÷ 1 = ₹125.
  - Engine scans Leg 2's available strikes for that day and picks whichever one
  has closing premium closest to ₹125.

  If Leg 1 and Leg 2 were the same expiry type (e.g. both Weekly), the divisor
  is just 1 — target = ref premium ÷ lots, no splitting.

  If Leg 2 had 2 lots instead of 1: target = 500 ÷ 4 ÷ 2 = ₹62.5 — more lots
  means each lot's target premium shrinks, so total premium committed on Leg 2
  tracks Leg 1's premium roughly evenly regardless of lot size.

  The idea: spread a reference leg's premium evenly across however many
  smaller-cycle contracts it covers, scaled down further per lot.

● Good — everything I need. Here's the plan.

  What "the project" actually is

  - Code: already a git repo on GitHub (PatelNandini30/Intraday-Algo) — just git
  clone.
  - Data: /data/intraday, separate from git, 12.6GB of it is actually needed
  (NIFTY, MIDCPNIFTY, NIFTYMIDCAP100 snapshot dirs + _manifest.sqlite*). Skip
  _staging_manan, _clean_parquet, _src_*.csv, _manifest.db — ~1GB of leftover
  import scratch that nothing in the code even reads (verified with grep, zero
  references).

  Since you're on the same LAN, this is a one-time local copy — no cloud upload
  needed.

  Steps for your friend's PC

  1. Prereqs (Rust + Node, matching what this box needs):
  curl https://sh.rustup.rs -sSf | sh    # Rust toolchain
  # Node 22 for the frontend build (same as this repo's frontend/ requires)

  2. Clone the code:
  git clone https://github.com/PatelNandini30/Intraday-Algo.git ~/intraday-algo

  3. Copy the data over LAN (run from this PC, pushing to theirs — swap in their
  IP; needs SSH enabled on their end):
  sudo rsync -avh --progress \
    --exclude='_staging_manan' --exclude='_clean_parquet' \
    --exclude='_src_*.csv' --exclude='_manifest.db' \
    /data/intraday/ friend@<their-lan-ip>:/data/intraday/
  On gigabit LAN that's ~2 min; on WiFi, more like 15–20 min. If either PC lacks
  SSH, doing it via a USB drive is just as valid — same exclude list.

  4. Give them their own systemd service (same shape as this PC's, just on their
  machine):
  # ~/.config/systemd/user/iserver.service
  [Unit]
  Description=intraday-algo iserver
  [Service]
  WorkingDirectory=/home/<friend>/intraday-algo
  Environment=INTRADAY_DATA_DIR=/data/intraday INTRADAY_GATE_MB=3000
  INTRADAY_PORT=8200
  ExecStart=/home/<friend>/intraday-algo/target/release/iserver
  Restart=always
  RestartSec=2
  [Install]
  WantedBy=default.target
  Then ./deploy.sh --ui on their box builds + starts it, and they hit their own
  http://localhost:8200.

  Working in parallel afterward

  - Code: normal git — each of you branches, pushes, PRs/merges through GitHub.
  No conflict since you each run your own binary.
  - Data: it's read-mostly (backtests only read it). No live sync needed. The
  only time you need to re-rsync is if one of you imports fresh CSVs via the
  migrate tool — then re-run the same rsync command (it's incremental, so a
  re-sync after an import only transfers what changed, not the full 13GB again).
  - Each of you is fully independent — no shared server, no single point of
  failure, no need to keep this PC running for them.

  Want me to also write this up as a SETUP.md in the repo so it's not just in
  chat?

      621 +/// TIME_VALUE strike pick (AlgoTest EOD `native/simulate.rs::
          +StrikeSel::TimeValue`):                                       
      622 +/// CE intrinsic = `max(spot−strike, 0)`, PE = `max(strike−spo
          +t, 0)`; TV =                                                  
      623 +/// `close − intrinsic` (`÷ spot × 100` if `pct_units`). Candi
          +dates split                                                   
      624 +/// OTM/ITM by intrinsic sign, each ordered strictly outward f
          +rom ATM (ties:                                                
      625 +/// CE prefers the higher strike, PE the lower), and the walk 
          +stops at the                                                  
      626 +/// first point `|TV − target|` (rounded to 6dp, to make float
          +-noise ties                                                   
      627 +/// register as ties) stops improving — it never scans past a 
          +local optimum                                                 
      628 +/// hunting for a "better" far match. On an exact tie a non-ne
          +gative TV beats                                               
      629 +/// a negative one, ranked ABOVE ATM distance. `moneyness == A
          +ny` walks both                                                
      630 +/// sides and keeps the smaller-distance winner (sign tie-brea
          +k, then                                                       
      631 +/// closer-to-ATM, as the final tiebreaks).                   
      632 +#[allow(clippy::too_many_arguments)]                          
      633 +fn pick_time_value(                                           
      634 +    chain: &[(i64, i64)],                                     
      635 +    atm_strike: i64,                                          
      636 +    spot_x100: i64,                                           
      637 +    opt: OptType,                                             
      638 +    target: f64,                                              
      639 +    moneyness: crate::strategy::Moneyness,                    
      640 +    range_pct: f64,                                           
      641 +    pct_units: bool,                                          
      642 +) -> Option<i64> {                                            
      643 +    use crate::strategy::Moneyness;                           
      644 +                                                              
      645 +    let mut otm: Vec<(i64, f64)> = Vec::new();                
      646 +    let mut itm: Vec<(i64, f64)> = Vec::new();                
      647 +    for &(s, close) in chain {                                
      648 +        if range_pct > 0.0 && ((s as f64 / spot_x100 as f64 - 
          +1.0).abs() * 100.0) > range_pct {                             
      649 +            continue;                                         
      650 +        }                                                     
      651 +        let intrinsic = match opt {                           
      652 +            OptType::Ce => (spot_x100 - s).max(0),            
      653 +            OptType::Pe => (s - spot_x100).max(0),            
      654 +        };                                                    
      655 +        let tv_x100 = close - intrinsic;                      
      656 +        let tv = if pct_units {                               
      657 +            tv_x100 as f64 / spot_x100 as f64 * 100.0         
      658 +        } else {                                              
      659 +            tv_x100 as f64 / 100.0                            
      660 +        };                                                    
      661 +        if intrinsic > 0 {                                    
      662 +            itm.push((s, tv));                                
      663 +        } else {                                              
      664 +            otm.push((s, tv));                                
      665 +        }                                                     
      666 +    }                                                         
      667 +                                                              
      668 +    let order = |a: i64, b: i64| {                            
      669 +        (a - atm_strike).abs().cmp(&(b - atm_strike).abs()).th
          +en(match opt {                                                
      670 +            OptType::Ce => b.cmp(&a),                         
      671 +            OptType::Pe => a.cmp(&b),                         
      672 +        })                                                    
      673 +    };                                                        
      674 +    let q = |d: f64| (d * 1.0e6).round() / 1.0e6;             
      675 +    let walk = |mut seq: Vec<(i64, f64)>| -> Option<(f64, i64,
          + f64)> {                                                      
      676 +        seq.sort_by(|a, b| order(a.0, b.0));                  
      677 +        let mut best: Option<(f64, i64, f64)> = None;         
      678 +        for (s, tv) in seq {                                  
      679 +            let d = q((tv - target).abs());                   
      680 +            match best {                                      
      681 +                None => best = Some((d, s, tv)),              
      682 +                Some((bd, _, btv)) => {                       
      683 +                    if d < bd {                               
      684 +                        best = Some((d, s, tv));              
      685 +                    } else {                                  
      686 +                        if d == bd && tv >= 0.0 && btv < 0.0 {
      687 +                            best = Some((d, s, tv));          
      688 +                        }                                     
      689 +                        break;                                
      690 +                    }                                         
      691 +                }                                             
      692 +            }                                                 
      693 +        }                                                     
      694 +        best                                                  
      695 +    };                                                        
      696 +                                                              
      697 +    let mut hits: Vec<(f64, i64, f64)> = Vec::new();          
      698 +    if !matches!(moneyness, Moneyness::Itm) {                 
      699 +        if let Some(h) = walk(otm) {                          
      700 +            hits.push(h);                                     
      701 +        }                                                     
      702 +    }                                                         
      703 +    if !matches!(moneyness, Moneyness::Otm) {                 
      704 +        if let Some(h) = walk(itm) {                          
      705 +            hits.push(h);                                     
      706 +        }                                                     
      707 +    }                                                         
      708 +    hits.sort_by(|a, b| {                                     
      709 +        a.0.partial_cmp(&b.0)                                 
      710 +            .unwrap_or(std::cmp::Ordering::Equal)             
      711 +            .then_with(|| (a.2 < 0.0).cmp(&(b.2 < 0.0)))      
      712 +            .then_with(|| (a.1 - atm_strike).abs().cmp(&(b.1 -
          + atm_strike).abs()))                                          
      713 +    });                                                       
      714 +    hits.into_iter().next().map(|x| x.1)                      
      715 +}                                                             
      716 +                                                              
      717  /// A premium amount (`*100`) for a basis and a pct/points spe
           c. Underlying
      718  /// bases are handled on the spot path in `scan_exit`, not her
           e (return 0).
      719  fn amount_x100(basis_x100: i64, basis: StopBasis, value: f64) 
           -> i64 {

● Summary

  Time Value strike selection is now live, ported from the EOD AlgoTest engine's
  exact algorithm:

  Engine (crates/iengine/src/strategy.rs, engine.rs):
  - New StrikeMode::TimeValue { target, cmp, moneyness, range_pct, pct_units } —
  CE intrinsic = max(spot−strike,0), PE = max(strike−spot,0), TV = close − 
  intrinsic (or ÷spot×100 for percent mode).
  - Nearest: walks outward from ATM per side (OTM/ITM), stops at the first point
  the match stops improving — never scans past a local optimum for a
  numerically "better" but far-ITM/OTM match.
  - Gte/Lte: nearest qualifying strike walking outward (not the last one still
  qualifying) — since TV peaks near ATM, this almost always resolves to ATM
  whenever the floor is modest.
  - Cap fallback: if nothing clears the gte/lte bar, falls back to the nearest
  pick within the same (range-capped) set rather than dropping the trade.
  - Sign tie-break: on an exact distance tie, non-negative TV beats negative,
  ranked above ATM-distance.
  - Didn't need EOD's separate "tradeable" filter — this engine drops
  padded/untraded minute rows at ingest, so contract_bar already only returns
  real prints.

  Tests: 4 new engine tests using AlgoTest EOD's own fixture numbers (spot
  25202.35, gap 100) — crossing-stop, sign-tie-break, GTE-nearest-qualifying,

❯ now see we need to add the new toggle of per leg volume or turnover so first  
  tell me are we having that field in data?                                     

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● Confirmed. Here's the data picture:

  Volume — yes, already fully in the pipeline. Source CSVs have a
  Volume/Contracts column (crates/idata/src/csvio.rs:63), it's parsed into every
  RawBar, and it's stored per-minute per-contract in the binary snapshot format
  (Bar.volume, crates/idata/src/snapshot.rs:44,59) that the engine reads via
  contract_bar(). It's just not used by any strike/filter logic yet — the field
  is there, wired end-to-end, sitting unused.

  Open Interest — the source CSV header actually has an Open Interest column
  (Ticker,Date,Time,Expiry Date,Open,High,Low,Close,Volume,Open Interest,Padding
  Flag — see the doc comment at csvio.rs:3), but it's currently dropped at 
  ingest: Cols never resolves it, RawBar/Bar have no field for it, and the
  on-disk snapshot format is hardcoded to 5 fields per minute (FIELDS_PER_MIN = 
  5 — O/H/L/C/V, snapshot.rs:44). To use OI you'd need to touch the ingest
  columns, both struct definitions, the binary format constant, and re-migrate
  all the .its2 snapshots (13GB) from source CSVs — not a small change.

  Turnover (rupee value traded) — not a raw column at all in these vendor CSVs;
  it'd have to be derived (volume × price, optionally × lot_size), there's
  nothing to "add" from source data.

  So: if the toggle is volume-based, it's a same-day feature — just new filter
  logic reading a field that's already there. If it needs open interest, that's
  a real data-pipeline + re-migration project first. Which one did you mean?

  to 10:15 we will aggregrate the sum so tell me your complete understanding    
  with live trade example if doubt ask all the possible questions               

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● My understanding

  A new per-leg toggle (in Leg-Wise Roll's leg controls, alongside Entry/Exit
  T-n, Wait & Trade, etc.) that gates entry on recent trading activity in the
  leg's own contract, with a fallback that aggregates over a trailing window
  when the exact entry-minute has no print.

  Two data facts that matter here, confirmed from the code:
  1. Volume (contracts traded) is already captured per-minute per-contract in
  the data (Bar.volume) — nothing new to ingest. Turnover (₹ value traded) is
  not a raw field anywhere in this pipeline; it would have to be derived as
  volume × premium on the fly. Since you used both words, I need to know which
  one you actually mean as the number compared against your threshold.
  2. Important architecture fact: in this engine, a minute with zero trades 
  doesn't produce a bar with volume=0 — it produces no bar at all
  (untraded/padding rows are dropped at ingest, confirmed in csvio.rs). So
  "10:15 turnover is 0" and "10:15 has no recorded print" are the same event
  here — there's no way to distinguish "traded 0 contracts" from "didn't trade."
  I'll treat any missing minute as contributing 0 to the window sum.

  Live example, mirroring yours exactly:

  Leg-Wise Roll, entry scheduled at 10:50 (pushed later than the leg's normal
  entry time by a spot-adjustment re-entry or similar). User sets window = 360 
  minutes, threshold = say 500 contracts.

  - Naive lookback would be 10:50 − 360min = 04:50 — nonsense, pre-market. In
  bar-index terms: entry bar = (10:50 − 09:15) = 95; 95 − 360 = −265, a negative
  index.
  - The bug you're flagging: if that negative index isn't clamped, it could
  wrap/underflow into reading the previous trading day's snapshot file entirely
  — a completely different day's data pretending to be "this morning's volume."
  - Correct behavior: clamp the window start to session open same day. So the
  effective window becomes [09:15, 10:50] — only 95 minutes of real data, even
  though 360 was requested. Never opens the prior day's file for this check,
  full stop.

  Then, at 10:50 (or whatever the checked minute is): if that contract has no 
  print at 10:50 (→ volume 0 by the fact above), sum every real minute bar's
  volume from 09:15 through 10:50 (the clamped window) and compare that sum to
  the threshold.

  Questions I need answered before touching the engine

● User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · What number gets compared to the user's threshold? → Volume (contracts) —
     Recommended
     · What does the check actually gate? → Gate the entry (Recommended)
     · When does the windowed backward-sum kick in? → Only when the exact minute
     has no print (Recommended)
     · If the sum still doesn't clear the threshold even after using the full 
     (session-clamped) window — what happens? → Skip this cycle (Recommended)

      2933 +/// Per-leg volume filter (`Leg::vol_filter_on`): does `strik
           +e` clear the                                                 
      2934 +/// user's liquidity bar at `entry_bar`? A real print with `v
           +olume > 0` at                                                
      2935 +/// the exact minute passes immediately, no threshold check. 
           +Otherwise sums                                               
      2936 +/// real volume backward over `vol_window_min` minutes (bars)
           + and compares                                                
      2937 +/// to `vol_threshold`; the window start is clamped to bar 0 
           +(session open)                                               
      2938 +/// via `saturating_sub`, so it can never read into the previ
           +ous day — each                                               
      2939 +/// day is its own snapshot, bar indices are always relative 
           +to THIS day's                                                
      2940 +/// 09:15.                                                   
      2941 +fn volume_gate_ok(                                           
      2942 +    snap: &SnapshotReader,                                   
      2943 +    exp_days: i32,                                           
      2944 +    strike: i64,                                             
      2945 +    opt: OptType,                                            
      2946 +    entry_bar: u16,                                          
      2947 +    leg: &Leg,                                               
      2948 +) -> bool {

           + entry_bar) {                                                
      2950 +        if b.volume > 0 {                                    
      2951 +            return true;                                     
      2952 +        }                                                    
      2953 +    }                                                        
      2954 +    let start = entry_bar.saturating_sub(leg.vol_window_min a
           +s u16);                                                      
      2955 +    let mut sum: i64 = 0;                                    
      2956 +    for bar in start..=entry_bar {                           
      2957 +        if let Some(b) = snap.contract_bar(exp_days, strike, 
           +opt, bar) {                                                  
      2958 +            sum += b.volume as i64;                          
      2959 +        }                                                    
      2960 +    }                                                        
      2961 +    sum >= leg.vol_threshold

● Confirmed working live in plain Positional mode with the filter on.

  Summary

  Built the per-leg Volume Filter and corrected its scope per your feedback
  (initially built it Leg-Wise-Roll-only, you caught it should be a general leg
  control across all trade types):

  - Engine (Leg.vol_filter_on/vol_threshold/vol_window_min in strategy.rs, gate
  in price_one): checks the leg's wanted strike (before any illiquidity-shift)
  at the entry minute. A real print with volume > 0 passes instantly, threshold
  never consulted. Only when that minute has no print does it sum real volume
  backward over the window, clamped to session open (saturating_sub) — never
  reaches the previous day's data, however large the window. Failing skips the
  whole cycle.
  - Applies in any trade type now — Intraday, BTST, Positional, Leg-Wise Roll —
  not just Leg-Wise Roll.
  - 4 engine tests (exact-minute pass, windowed-sum pass + session clamp,
  insufficient-sum skip, and same behavior confirmed outside Leg-Wise Roll).
  Full suite: 57/17/32/8 green.
  - UI: "Volume Filter" toggle now sits with the other always-visible leg
  toggles (after Spot Adjustment), same place in every trade type — matches the
  screenshot you flagged.
  - Live-verified against real NIFTY data in both Leg-Wise Roll and plain
  Positional mode.

  Deployed and confirmed live.

● Two files. Let's read both fully to understand the approach.

  Read 3 files, ran 5 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● Read both files off the SMB share. Here's my understanding.

  How the friend's system (StockMock-Copy) works

  It's a nightly cron (daily_update_cron.sh → daily_update.py, 19:00) that
  incrementally refreshes their Parquet-based NIFTY store from the exact same
  SMB share (192.168.4.50/share/Manan Pujara/Breezeapi data/...) our own icli 
  migrate already reads from. Key mechanisms:

  1. Cron-environment fix: the SMB share is a GVFS mount that lives in the
  desktop login session, not the system mount table — cron's near-empty
  environment can't see /run/user/1000/gvfs/... without XDG_RUNTIME_DIR +
  DBUS_SESSION_BUS_ADDRESS pointing at that session's bus. The script checks the
  mount exists up front and hard-fails (exit 2) rather than silently
  "succeeding" with nothing ingested.
  2. Watermark-based incrementality: a JSON state file tracks the mtime of the
  last successfully processed file; each run only looks at source files newer
  than that.
  3. Two-phase scan: cheap listdir() (names only, filtered to non-stale
  expiries) before the expensive per-file stat() — avoids timing out on a
  30k-file SMB directory.
  4. Stage locally, then ingest — never parses directly off the flaky network
  mount.
  5. Per-expiry invalidation — only touched contracts get reprocessed, not a
  full rebuild.
  6. Failure isolation: each step tracked independently; nothing aborts the
  whole run, but the watermark only advances on a fully clean run (so a partial
  failure retries the same files next time, never silently drops data).
  7. Rebuild catalog + restart the server, but only if something actually
  ingested.
  8. Dated logs, 30-day retention, --dry-run support.

  Why porting this 1:1 would be over-engineering here

  Checked our own pipeline against theirs — we're structurally in a better
  position already:

  - icli migrate is already manifest-incremental: without --force it skips any
  day already recorded in _manifest.sqlite (migrate.rs:361). That's their
  watermark file, already built in.
  - It already writes expiries.json as part of the normal run (per its own doc
  comment) — no separate calendar-refresh step needed.
  - It already retries once on an empty directory listing (migrate.rs — "SMB
  hiccup?" retry), same defensiveness as their scan.
  - No restart needed at all — I checked DataStore::open(): it re-scans the
  snapshots directory fresh on every request (not cached at server startup), so
  new .its2 files are visible on the very next backtest with zero server
  restart. Their system needs a restart because their catalog is loaded once at
  process start; ours isn't.

  So the actual gap is much smaller than their script: just a cron wrapper (same
  GVFS env-var fix, since this is the identical mount setup) that calls icli 
  migrate per symbol with a safe lookback window, plus logging. No staging copy,
  no watermark file, no catalog rebuild, no restart — matching this project's
  own bash-script conventions (deploy.sh/start.sh) rather than introducing
  Python into an all-Rust project.

  Before I build it:

● Let me reproduce this directly against the engine to isolate what's actually
  happening.

  Read 1 file, ran 4 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)
● Found it. In engine.rs's leg-wise loop, the guard if (exit_day, xb) < 
  (entry_day, eb) lets entry_bar == exit_bar through to price_one, which then
  successfully computes a zero-duration row — but a downstream line silently
  drops any row where exit isn't strictly after entry, with no skip recorded at 
  all. That's your "0 0": the trade actually got computed, then vanished without
  a trace.

● Update(~/Downloads/intraday-algo/crates/iengine/src/engine.rs)

❯ what was the issue?                                                           

● The bug

  When you set Leg-Wise Roll's Entry Time equal to the Exit/Square-off Time (so
  a leg's entry and exit land on the exact same minute), the engine actually
  computed the trade successfully — then threw it away silently, with zero
  explanation in the tradesheet or skip log. That's exactly the "0 0" you saw.

  Root cause: two pieces of code disagreed about whether "entry minute == exit
  minute" is valid:
  - The loop that decides whether to even attempt the trade only rejected cases
  where the exit was strictly before entry — equal was allowed through.
  - A separate line further down, meant to clip trades right at the backtest's
  end date, quietly drops any resulting row where exit isn't strictly after
  entry — which equal minutes also trips.

  So a same-minute trade sailed past the first check, got computed, then got
  wiped out by the second one — with no skip ever recorded, because that second
  check was never designed to be the "this is invalid" gate, just a
  boundary-clamp cleanup.

  Fix: added an explicit check right where the loop first sees entry_bar ==
  exit_bar, rejecting it immediately with a real skip reason ("entry and exit
  both resolve to <date> at the same minute — zero-duration trade rejected")
  instead of letting it fall through to the silent drop.

  This is unrelated to the "Re-entry Rollover disabled" thing you also flagged —
  that one's intentional, it's mutually exclusive with Leg-Wise Roll by design
  (you can't have two different rollover mechanisms active at once), and the UI
  already labels it that way.

  Test suite's finishing in the background — I'll deploy and re-verify against
  the exact scenario next.

● My understanding

  You want a per-leg toggle for Leg-Wise Roll that reproduces exactly what
  Positional's Re-entry Rollover already does — the tradesheet you pasted:

  1  Entry 2025-01-29 → Exit 2025-02-25, Expiry 2025-02-27, ROLLOVER
  2  Entry 2025-02-25 → Exit 2025-03-26, Expiry 2025-03-27, ROLLOVER

  Each row's entry date equals the previous row's exit date — a continuous
  chain. The mechanic behind it (confirmed by reading the actual Positional
  code): for each pair of consecutive expiries (call them E_k and E_k+1):
  - Entry is timed off E_k — "this week's" expiry
  - The contract actually traded is E_k+1 — "next week's" expiry
  - Exit/roll is timed off E_k+1 (the contract you're actually holding), not E_k

  So you enter today using this week's schedule, but you're already holding next
  week's contract, and you cut it near next week's expiry — then the chain
  continues, E_k+1 becoming the new E_k for the following cycle.

  What's different from what I already built: my earlier Entry/Exit T-n work
  made each leg's entry and exit both relative to the same contract's own expiry
  (enter and exit within the same week, independently per cycle — which you
  separately confirmed you wanted too, for a different scenario). This is a
  second, distinct mode — not a replacement — for legs that instead want the
  continuous forward-roll behavior, just scoped to one leg instead of the whole
  strategy.

  I've just wired this into the engine as a new per-leg roll_forward toggle: off
  = your existing same-cycle model (unchanged), on = this
  Re-entry-Rollover-style chain, independently per leg. Compiling and testing it
  now — will report back once verified against real data.

❯ continue                                                                      

● Summary

  Built and verified the per-leg Roll Forward feature for Leg-Wise Roll — same
  "hold the next contract from day one" mechanic as Positional's Re-entry
  Rollover, scoped independently per leg:

  Engine (new Leg.roll_forward field):
  - Entry anchored to the CURRENT cycle's own expiry, but the contract actually
  traded (and Exit T-n) is the NEXT one — chain continues automatically, next
  cycle's anchor becoming this cycle's traded contract.
  - Fixed two real bugs found via direct parity-testing against Positional:
    a. Same-minute roll: a chained cycle landing on the prior cycle's exact exit
  day now opens at that exit's actual minute, not the configured entry bar —
  without this, cycles could structurally overlap (entering before the prior
  position technically closed).
    b. Data-horizon guard: won't start a cycle whose traded contract expires
  beyond the last loaded trading day — matches Positional's own if lead > 
  last_day break, previously leg-wise would start it anyway and let the
  range-end clamp truncate it into a bogus extra trade.
  - Live parity check: same single-leg config run through
  Positional+Re-entry-Rollover vs Leg-Wise-Roll+roll_forward produced a
  byte-for-byte identical diff (entry/exit dates, times, strikes, prices) —
  confirmed with diff, zero output.
  - Multi-leg independence confirmed live: two legs with different T-n (T-1 vs
  T-2) on roll_forward run fully independent chains, each correctly using its
  own prior exit for same-minute rolls.
  - New regression test covering chaining, same-minute-roll, and the
  data-horizon guard. Full suite: 59/33/9/17, all green.

  UI: "Roll Forward" toggle added under Leg-Wise Roll's Entry/Exit T-n block,
  confirmed rendering correctly via live screenshot.

  Also from earlier in this session: confirmed the "0 0" bug (same-minute
  entry/exit silently vanishing) is fixed and live; the nightly SMB data-refresh
  script is written/tested but not yet installed to cron (paused pending your
  confirmation); flagged that per-leg spot-adjustment is currently unsupported
  in Leg-Wise Roll at all — separate follow-up if you want that "same contract
  vs next contract" re-entry choice built.

● 60/33/9/17, all green. Already deployed with the final fix.

  Answer: yes, now it does — but it didn't when you asked

  Your question caught a real, pre-existing bug (not just in my new roll_forward
  work — it also affected the already-shipped per-cycle Entry/Exit T-n
  feature). Root cause: NextWeekly/NextMonthly need an extra "which contract is
  actually next" resolution step (leg_traded_expiry → traded_contract_expiry)
  that Leg-Wise Roll was never applying — it only worked by accident for
  Weekly/Monthly, where that resolution happens to be a no-op.

  Fixing it took three rounds, each caught by re-verifying against real
  Positional data as ground truth rather than trusting my own code-reading:
  1. Added the missing per-leg-type resolution
  (leg_traded_expiry/traded_contract_expiry) for both modes.
  2. Found the "same-minute roll" override was firing in the plain per-cycle
  mode too, where Positional's own plain path never does that — gated it to
  roll_forward only.
  3. Found I'd invented an "advance from exit_anchor" rule from a stale memory
  of different code — the real rule advances by exactly one tenor step from the
  anchor itself, independent of the leg's own Next*-skip. Fixed, and corrected
  the matching "don't start past last_day" guard to check the right variable.

  Final verified state, live against real NIFTY data, single leg, same config
  both sides:
  - roll_forward mode: Weekly, NextWeekly, Monthly, NextMonthly — all
  byte-for-byte identical to Positional's Re-entry Rollover.
  - Plain per-cycle mode: all four match for the entire trade body; only
  pre-existing, already-accepted boundary differences remain (leg-wise clamps a
  trailing cycle instead of refusing it — that's an established design choice
  from before today, not something this fix touched).

  Added a dedicated NextWeekly regression test (locks in the "three tenor steps"
  resolution). Full suite: 60/33/9/17, all green.

✻ Worked for 21m 8s

❯  now tell me how existing is working?                                         

● How the existing (per-cycle, non-roll_forward) mode works now

  Each leg runs its own independent chain, one cycle per calendar tenor step
  (week or month), completely separate from every other leg. For each cycle:

  1. Anchor (ak) — always a plain weekly/monthly point, e.g. "this week's
  expiry" — regardless of whether the leg is Weekly or NextWeekly.

  2. Where entry/exit/contract land, per expiry type:

  ┌─────────────┬───────────────────┬──────────────────┬────────────────────┐
  │    Type     │ Entry anchored to │ Exit anchored to │  Contract traded   │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ Weekly      │ ak                │ ak               │ ak (same contract) │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ Monthly     │ ak                │ ak               │ ak (same contract) │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ NextWeekly  │ ak                │ ak + 1 week      │ ak + 2 weeks       │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ NextMonthly │ ak                │ ak + 1 month     │ ak + 2 months      │
  └─────────────┴───────────────────┴──────────────────┴────────────────────┘

  So a NextWeekly leg naturally holds across a week boundary every single cycle
  — that's the leg's own "Next" meaning, nothing to do with the roll_forward
  toggle. A Weekly leg stays self-contained within one week's own contract.

  Concrete example, verified live: NextWeekly, entry/exit both T-1 — anchor =
  the weekly expiring Jan 9 → entry Jan 8 (T-1 before that), exit Jan 15 (T-1
  before the next weekly, Jan 16), contract actually traded expires Jan 23 (one
  week further still).

  3. Advancing to the next cycle — the anchor always moves forward by exactly
  one plain tenor step (one week, or one month), regardless of type. That's what
  keeps the schedule's cadence steady at "once a week" or "once a month" even
  though NextWeekly/NextMonthly look further ahead for what they actually trade.

  4. Entry timing — always uses the leg's configured entry time. Unlike
  roll_forward, there's no "same-minute roll": even if a cycle's entry happens
  to land on the exact day a previous cycle exited, it still enters at the
  configured clock time, not the prior exit's minute. (Verified: this matches
  Positional's own plain, non-rollover behavior exactly.)

  5. Boundary/data-horizon handling — this is the one deliberate difference from
  roll_forward: if a cycle's own natural window runs past your backtest's to
  date (or the data horizon), this mode still starts the cycle and clamps its
  exit at the boundary, rather than refusing to start it at all. roll_forward
  refuses outright, matching Positional's rollover exactly. This was already an
  established, tested design choice from before today (confirmed by an existing
  test where a Monthly leg's own expiry sits past to and still produces one
  clamped trade) — I left it as-is rather than changing it.

  Live-verified byte-for-byte against real Positional data for the entire trade
  body, all four expiry types — differences only at that one accepted boundary
  case.

✻ Worked for 1m 4s

※ recap: Fixed Leg-Wise Roll's expiry handling across Weekly/NextWeekly/Monthly/NextMonthly, in both per-cycle and roll_forward modes; roll_forward now matches Positional byte-for-byte on all four. Just explained how the existing per-cycle mode behaves now, waiting on your next steps. (disable recaps in /config)

● Found it — this is the Volume Filter, not a Roll Forward/engine bug. You've
  set vol_window_min = 0, which means zero lookback tolerance: it only checks
  the exact entry minute's own print, with no fallback window at all. The actual
  skip reasons for all 4 missing months:

  2024-05-29: strike 23800 traded below 100 contracts (last 0 min)
  2024-06-26: strike 24600 traded below 100 contracts (last 0 min)
  2024-07-24: strike 25200 traded below 100 contracts (last 0 min)
  2024-08-28: strike 25700 traded below 100 contracts (last 0 min)

  Your strike criteria (Time Value nearest to 0, ITM only) tends to pick the
  deepest-ITM strike with the least remaining time value — those are exactly the
  strikes most likely to go a whole minute (or more) without a single trade.
  With window = 0, there's no forgiveness: if that exact minute has zero prints,
  the whole month gets skipped outright — and this happened 4 months in a row
  before a September cycle finally landed on a strike that had volume at that
  precise minute.

  This is the filter working exactly as configured, not a bug. Your options:
  1. Increase the window (e.g. 15 min) — gives it a real chance to sum recent
  volume instead of requiring an exact-minute print
  2. Lower the threshold below 100 contracts
  3. Turn off Volume Filter for this leg if you don't need it with this
  particular strike criteria

  Want me to just re-run this with a wider window to confirm those 4 months come
  back?

● Direct answer: no, there was no turnover at 3:15 that minute — confirmed 
  against the raw data file itself, not just the engine's self-report.

  Pulled the exact bar for strike 23800 PE, expiry 2024-05-30, at 15:15 on
  2024-05-29:

  open: 1052.50, high: 1052.50, low: 1052.50, close: 1052.50, volume: 0

  Open=High=Low=Close identical — that's the signature of a stale 
  carried-forward quote, not a real trade. And I checked every other PE strike
  with any print at that exact minute that day: the only nonzero ones were
  strike 23700 (volume 75) and 24000 (volume 25) — both still well under your
  100-contract threshold. So it wasn't a strike-selection fluke; 15:15 was just
  a genuinely thin minute for deep-ITM PE strikes that day.

  One thing this surfaced worth flagging: I'd earlier assumed this engine never
  stores a real bar with volume=0 (since untraded/padded minutes get dropped
  entirely at ingest) — that assumption turned out to be wrong. This specific
  vendor feed can carry a stale zero-volume print that isn't caught by the
  padding-flag filter. I corrected the doc comment in the code to reflect this
  (it now explicitly notes that "no bar" and "a real bar with volume=0" are two
  distinct cases the gate treats the same way). This actually validates the
  Volume Filter feature's design — it's catching a real, confirmed data-quality
  issue, not a phantom one.

● Found it, and this is a real, useful finding — strike 24000 DOES have real 
  turnover (350 contracts) at that exact minute, clearing your 100-contract
  threshold easily:

  2024-05-29, contract expiry 2024-06-27:
    wanted strike 23800 (Time Value picked this): volume 0  → fails, cycle
  skipped
    strike 24000 (just 200 pts away):              volume 350 → would have
  passed

  So the real issue isn't that turnover doesn't exist that day — it's that the
  Time Value (nearest, target 0, ITM only) criteria specifically picked strike
  23800 (deep ITM, stale zero-volume print), while a strike barely 200 points
  away had plenty of real activity. The Volume Filter checks only the exact
  strike the Strike Criteria already resolved — if that one fails, the whole
  cycle is rejected outright. It does not fall back to a nearby, more liquid
  strike like 24000.

  This matches a design choice you confirmed earlier when I first built this
  feature: "gate the entry" (check the already-resolved strike, reject if it
  fails) rather than "filter the candidates" (only pick among strikes that
  already clear the volume bar). Given this concrete case, do you want me to
  change that — so instead of rejecting the whole cycle, it falls back to the
  nearest strike that does clear the volume threshold?

● User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · When the wanted strike (from Time Value/whatever criteria) fails the 
     Volume Filter, should it fall back to the nearest strike that DOES clear 
     the threshold, instead of rejecting the whole cycle? → Yes, fall back to 
     nearest liquid strike (Recommended)

● Let me look at shift_to_tradeable's existing walking convention to build a
  consistent fallback.

           +(entry_snap.as_ref(), exp_days, want_strike, leg.opt_type, en
           +try_bar, leg) {                                              
      3872 +        match shift_to_volume_ok(entry_snap.as_ref(), exp_day
           +s, entry_bar, leg.opt_type, want_strike, sched_spot, leg) {  
      3873 +            Some(s) => s,                                    
      3874 +            None => {                                        
      3875 +                return note_skip(format!(                    
      3876 +                    "{tag}: volume filter — strike {} (and no
           +thing within the shift walk) traded below {} contracts (last 
           +{} min) on {entry_day}",                                     
      3877 +                    want_strike / 100,                       
      3878 +                    leg.vol_threshold,                       
      3879 +                    leg.vol_window_min                       
      3880 +                ));                                          
      3881 +            }                                                
      3882 +        }                                                    
      3883 +    } else {                                                 
      3884 +        want_strike                                          
      3885 +    };

            that day (deep-ITM
      3887      // contracts routinely don't). Shift to one that actually

      3179      None
      3180  }
      3181  
      3182 +/// Volume Filter fallback (`Leg::vol_filter_on`): when the w
           +anted strike                                                 
      3183 +/// fails `volume_gate_ok`, walk toward ATM — then, if ATM it
           +self also fails,                                             
      3184 +/// past it toward OTM (same convention and `MAX_SHIFT_STEPS`
           + bound as                                                    
      3185 +/// `shift_to_tradeable`) — for the nearest strike that DOES 
           +clear the                                                    
      3186 +/// threshold, instead of rejecting the whole cycle outright.
           + Confirmed                                                   
      3187 +/// against real data: a Time-Value-nearest pick can land on 
           +a genuinely                                                  
      3188 +/// dead deep-ITM strike while one just 200 points away has r
           +eal volume.                                                  
      3189 +fn shift_to_volume_ok(                                       
      3190 +    snap: &SnapshotReader,                                   
      3191 +    exp_days: i32,                                           
      3192 +    bar: u16,                                                
      3193 +    opt: OptType,                                            
      3194 +    strike: i64,                                             
      3195 +    spot_x100: i64,                                          
      3196 +    leg: &Leg,                                               
      3197 +) -> Option<i64> {                                           
      3198 +    let strikes = snap.strikes_for(exp_days);                
      3199 +    let si = match strikes.iter().position(|s| *s == strike) 
           +{                                                            
      3200 +        Some(i) => i,                                        
      3201 +        None => nearest_idx(strikes, strike)?,               
      3202 +    };                                                       
      3203 +    let ai = nearest_idx(strikes, spot_x100)?;               
      3204 +    let step: i32 = if si <= ai { 1 } else { -1 };           
      3205 +    let mut i = si as i32;                                   
      3206 +    loop {                                                   
      3207 +        let s = strikes[i as usize];                         
      3208 +        if volume_gate_ok(snap, exp_days, s, opt, bar, leg) {
      3209 +            return Some(s);                                  
      3210 +        }                                                    
      3211 +        if i == ai as i32 {                                  
      3212 +            break;                                           
      3213 +        }                                                    
      3214 +        i += step;                                           
      3215 +    }                                                        
      3216 +    let otm: i32 = match opt {                               
      3217 +        OptType::Ce => 1,                                    
      3218 +        OptType::Pe => -1,                                   
      3219 +    };                                                       
      3220 +    let mut j = ai as i32 + otm;                             
      3221 +    let mut steps = 0;                                       
      3222 +    while j >= 0 && (j as usize) < strikes.len() && steps < M
           +AX_SHIFT_STEPS {                                             
      3223 +        let s = strikes[j as usize];                         
      3224 +        if volume_gate_ok(snap, exp_days, s, opt, bar, leg) {
      3225 +            return Some(s);                                  
      3226 +        }                                                    
      3227 +        j += otm;                                            
      3228 +        steps += 1;                                          
      3229 +    }                                                        
      3230 +    None                                                     
      3231 +}                                                            
      3232 +

            t day's first bar.

            > Option<(NaiveDate, u16)> {
      3235      if (bar as usize) + 1 < BARS_PER_DAY as usize {

● Summary

  Root cause confirmed directly against raw data (not just the engine's
  self-report): strike 23800 genuinely had volume=0 at 15:15 — a stale
  carried-forward quote (O=H=L=C identical), not a real trade. But you were
  right to push on it — a nearby strike (24000, then later found others) had
  real turnover the whole time. The actual gap wasn't "no liquidity that day,"
  it was "the Volume Filter rejected the whole cycle instead of trying a nearby
  strike."

  Fixed: added shift_to_volume_ok — when the wanted strike fails the Volume
  Filter, it now walks toward ATM (then past it toward OTM if needed, same
  convention and step-cap as the existing shift_to_tradeable safety net) for the
  nearest strike that clears the threshold, instead of skipping the cycle
  outright.

  Result: your May 2024 → Aug 2024 gap is gone — all 30 monthly cycles across
  the full 2024–2026 range now produce trades, zero skips, fully continuous
  chain, matching the pattern from your pasted reference tradesheet.

  New regression test added (fallback walks to the correct nearby liquid strike
  rather than skipping), full suite 62/33/9/17 green, deployed and verified live
  against your exact real config.

  Also corrected a doc comment that turned out to be wrong: I'd assumed this
  engine could never have a real bar with volume=0 (only "no bar at all" for
  untraded minutes) — this investigation proved that assumption false, so the
  comment now reflects both cases.

  see if i selected time value 0 so why is it going seeing too far?             

● Good catch on the config — Volume Filter's hint text isn't showing this time,
  so it's off now; only Roll Forward is on, with range% now at 3. Let me run
  this exact config and check the actual spot-vs-strike distances directly.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Not a bug — the engine is correctly honoring your 3% cap on every single trade
  (distances cluster tightly between 1.4% and 2.99%, never once exceeding 3%).
  Here's why it still feels "too far":

  Time Value = premium − intrinsic value. For a Monthly PE, that value doesn't
  actually approach 0 until deep ITM — often 8–15%+ away from spot, depending on
  IV and time-to-expiry. Your 3% cap simply isn't deep enough to ever reach
  where TV genuinely crosses zero.

  So what happens every cycle: the "nearest to 0" walk keeps improving (TV keeps
  shrinking) all the way out to the edge of your 3% band, then stops there 
  because that's the boundary you set — not because it found TV≈0. That's why
  every single trade in your tradesheet sits almost exactly at 2.6–3.0% from
  spot: it's always hugging the cap, not actually finding a TV≈0 strike.

  This is the engine doing exactly what "Time Value nearest 0, capped at 3%"
  means — it's just that the combination of those two settings mostly reduces to
  "give me the deepest ITM strike within 3%," since true TV≈0 is unreachable
  within that band for a monthly contract. Two ways to fix the intent:
  1. Widen the range% substantially (try 10–15%) if you actually want it to find
  where TV crosses zero.
  2. Or accept it as "deepest-ITM-within-N%" if that's what you're actually
  after — in which case current behavior is correct.

  Want me to run this same leg with a wider range% (say 10%) so you can see
  where TV actually crosses zero for comparison?

● You're right to push on this — my Nearest walk does already consider negative
  TV (ported directly from AlgoTest's own rule: ranked purely on |TV−target|,
  sign doesn't disqualify it). The reason we never saw it here is specifically
  the 3% cap cutting the walk off before it ever reaches where TV actually
  crosses zero. Let me prove that by removing the cap and showing the real TV
  values, including negative ones.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Confirmed — with the cap removed, the walk now travels wherever it needs to
  and correctly lands on negative TV strikes whenever that's genuinely closer to
  0:

  2024-02-28: strike 22700, TV = -2.85   (distance 3.45% — past where the 3% cap
  cut it off before)
  2024-06-26: strike 24600, TV = -7.90
  2024-08-28: strike 25700, TV = -7.30
  2024-09-25: strike 26800, TV = -3.55
  2024-10-30: strike 25100, TV = -15.35

  This is exactly the AlgoTest crossing behavior: the walk moves outward from
  ATM, TV shrinks toward 0, crosses into negative territory, and it stops right
  at whichever side of that crossing is closer to |TV−target| — sign doesn't
  disqualify a candidate, only distance matters (matching the ported rule:
  "against target 0, TV −3 beats TV +5"). That's why 2024-04-24 lands on
  TV=+0.55 (barely positive, closer than any negative alternative) while
  2024-10-30 lands on TV=−15.35 (the negative side won there).

  So the mechanic was never broken — the 3% cap in your last config was just
  cutting the walk off before it could reach these crossing points, which is why
  every trade there clustered at the cap boundary instead of at the true TV≈0
  crossing.

※ recap: Working on intraday-algo's Leg-Wise Roll and strike-selection features; last fix confirmed Time Value's nearest-walk correctly picks negative TV strikes once the range cap is removed. Next: awaiting your direction on range% sizing or further checks. (disable recaps in /config)

❯ now in time value calcualtion i had selected nearest for strike criteria so   
  just tell me if i given 0 as input so it need to select strike price with 0   
  time value nearest right so will it consider a ITM PE which are trading at    
  discount to find strike nearest to 0 or only strike are eligible which has    
  time value?                                                                   

● Yes — a discounted (negative time value) ITM PE is fully eligible, and will be
  picked if it's genuinely the closest to 0. It is not restricted to only
  strikes with TV ≥ 0.

  The rule (ported directly from AlgoTest EOD, and just demonstrated live in the
  uncapped run above): every candidate strike is ranked purely by absolute 
  distance |TV − target|, regardless of sign. A strike trading at a ₹15 discount
  to its own intrinsic value (TV = −15) is treated exactly the same as one
  trading at a ₹15 premium (TV = +15) — both are "15 away from 0," and whichever
  one is actually closer wins, sign included.

  You already saw this happen in your own data:
  - 2024-10-30: strike 25100 selected with TV = −15.35 — a real
  discount-to-intrinsic strike, picked because nothing closer to 0 existed on
  the positive side either.
  - 2024-04-24: strike 23000 selected with TV = +0.55 — here the positive side
  happened to win because it was closer than any available negative-TV strike.

  So the walk doesn't stop at TV=0 and refuse to go further, and it doesn't
  filter out discounted strikes before comparing them — it keeps walking outward
  from ATM until the match stops improving, comparing every strike (premium or
  discount) purely on how close its TV sits to your target, and stops at
  whichever side of that zero-crossing is nearest.

  That's the surprising part worth flagging clearly: strike 23500 only has 25 
  contracts of volume — well under your 100 threshold. It still passes because
  of a rule you confirmed when we first built this feature: any nonzero volume 
  at the exact entry minute passes immediately, regardless of the threshold. The
  100-contract threshold only gets compared against the windowed sum as a
  fallback, and only when the exact minute is completely dead (volume = 0).
  Since 23500 had some real print (25 contracts, not zero), it passed outright —
  the threshold never actually got checked against it.

  So with these exact settings, the walk stops at the first strike with any real
  trade at all, not the first strike with ≥100 contracts. If you actually want
  a hard floor of 100 contracts enforced on every candidate (not just as a
  fallback when the exact minute is dead), that's a different rule than what's
  currently built — want me to change it so the threshold applies even when the
  minute has some nonzero volume?

● Two separate things are going on here — let me untangle both.

  First, 24000 is not actually the nearest to 0 — 23800 is. I already walked
  this by hand earlier:

  ┌────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │ Strike │         TV          │
  ├────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ 23700  │ 32.50               │
  ├────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ 23800  │ 9.30 ← nearest to 0 │
  ├────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ 23900  │ 10.95               │
  ├────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ 24000  │ −16.05              │
  └────────┴─────────────────────┘

  |9.30| < |16.05|, so 23800, not 24000, is the true nearest-to-zero strike.
  Without the Volume Filter, the engine correctly picks 23800 (I verified this
  earlier in this same conversation).

  Second — this is the real issue. With Volume Filter on, 23800 fails (0
  volume), so the engine falls back. But the fallback I built doesn't try "the
  next-nearest-TV candidate" (which would be 23900, then 24000, etc.) — it does
  something different: it walks from the failed strike toward ATM, looking for
  any strike with real volume, the same convention as the existing
  illiquid-strike safety net (shift_to_tradeable). Strike 23500 is what it hits
  first walking that direction (25 contracts, technically nonzero, so it passes
  instantly). 24000 is in the opposite direction from ATM, so that walk never
  even looks at it.

  So the real question is: what should the fallback actually search through when
  the wanted strike fails volume?

● User answered Claude's questions:

     the fallback try next? → Next-nearest Time Value candidate (Recommended)

  Value candidates specifically, not toward ATM. This is scoped to TimeValue
  mode (other strike modes keep the existing liquidity-walk fallback, since they
  don't have a natural "next-nearest" ranking).

      604      }
      605  }
      606  
      607 +/// Volume Filter fallback for `StrikeMode::TimeValue` specifi
          +cally: unlike                                                 
      608 +/// other modes (which fall back to `shift_to_volume_ok`'s ATM
          +-ward liquidity                                               
      609 +/// walk — see `price_one`), Time Value's fallback stays faith
          +ful to its OWN                                                
      610 +/// ranking instead of jumping toward ATM/liquidity regardless
          + of TV fit: it                                                
      611 +/// re-resolves the nearest candidate EXCLUDING every strike a
          +lready tried                                                  
      612 +/// and rejected, i.e. the next-nearest-to-target strike, one 
          +exclusion at a                                                
      613 +/// time, until one clears the volume gate. Confirmed against 
          +a real case: a                                                
      614 +/// strike 200 points closer to ATM had volume but a far worse
          + TV fit, while                                                
      615 +/// the genuinely next-best TV candidate (200 points the OTHER
          + way) was what                                                
      616 +/// the user actually wanted tried first.                     
      617 +#[allow(clippy::too_many_arguments)]                          
      618 +fn resolve_time_value_excluding(                              
      619 +    snap: &SnapshotReader,                                    
      620 +    exp_days: i32,                                            
      621 +    bar: u16,                                                 
      622 +    spot_x100: i64,                                           
      623 +    opt: OptType,                                             
      624 +    target: f64,                                              
      625 +    cmp: crate::strategy::TvCmp,                              
      626 +    moneyness: crate::strategy::Moneyness,                    
      627 +    range_pct: f64,                                           
      628 +    pct_units: bool,                                          
      629 +    gap_x100: i64,                                            
      630 +    exclude: &[i64],                                          
      631 +) -> Option<i64> {                                            
      632 +    let strikes = snap.strikes_for(exp_days);                 
      633 +    if strikes.is_empty() {                                   
      634 +        return None;                                          
      635 +    }                                                         
      636 +    let interval = if gap_x100 > 0 { gap_x100 } else { grid_in
          +terval(strikes) };                                            
      637 +    let atm_strike = snap_to_grid(strikes, atm_value(strikes, 
          +spot_x100, interval)?)?;                                      
      638 +    let on_grid = |s: i64| interval <= 0 || s % interval == 0;
      639 +    let build_chain = |grid: bool| -> Vec<(i64, i64)> {       
      640 +        strikes                                               
      641 +            .iter()                                           
      642 +            .copied()                                         
      643 +            .filter(|s| !grid || on_grid(*s))                 
      644 +            .filter(|s| !exclude.contains(s))                 
      645 +            .filter_map(|s| snap.contract_bar(exp_days, s, opt
          +, bar).map(|b| (s, b.close as i64)))                          
      646 +            .collect()                                        
      647 +    };                                                        
      648 +    let mut chain = build_chain(true);                        
      649 +    if chain.is_empty() {                                     
      650 +        chain = build_chain(false);                           
      651 +    }                                                         
      652 +    if chain.is_empty() {                                     
      653 +        return None;                                          
      654 +    }                                                         
      655 +    pick_time_value(&chain, atm_strike, spot_x100, opt, target
          +, cmp, moneyness, range_pct, pct_units)                       
      656 +}                                                             
      657 +                                                              
      658  /// CLOSEST_PREMIUM pick (EOD base.py:3292-3344): min |prem - 
           target|; on a tie,
      659  /// CE prefers the HIGHER strike, PE the LOWER strike.
      660  fn pick_closest_premium(chain: &[(i64, i64)], target_x100: i64
           , opt: OptType) -> Option<i64> {

  ATM-ward liquidity walk unchanged — this fix is scoped to TimeValue only,

❯ why are we using roll forward explain me with example                         

● Roll Forward makes a leg hold a continuous rolling position — never flat, 
  always in some contract — instead of independent, bracketed trades per cycle.

  The problem it solves

  Without Roll Forward, a leg's Entry T-n and Exit T-n are both measured against
  the same contract's own expiry. For a Monthly leg with Entry T-1 / Exit T-1,
  that means entry and exit both land on the exact same day (T-1 before that
  month's expiry) — a same-day, essentially zero-duration cycle. That's fine for
  strategies that genuinely only want a brief window near expiry each month,
  but it's not how a real "hold a short-premium position continuously" strategy
  behaves.

  With Roll Forward, using your own real config

  Monthly PE, Entry T-1, Exit T-1, Roll Forward ON — verified live earlier in
  this session:

  2024-01-24 → 2024-02-28   (holding the Feb contract, expiry 2024-02-29)
  2024-02-28 → 2024-03-27   (holding the Mar contract, expiry 2024-03-28)
  2024-03-27 → 2024-04-24   (holding the Apr contract, expiry 2024-04-25)

  Entry is T-1 before this month's expiry, but the contract actually held — and
  the exit — are anchored to next month's expiry instead. So on Jan 24, you're
  not buying a Jan-expiring contract that dies the same day — you're already
  holding the Feb contract, and you ride it until T-1 before its expiry, at
  which point you roll straight into March's contract. Each row's entry date
  equals the prior row's exit date — that's the "never flat" part.

  When to use which

  - Roll Forward off: independent per-cycle brackets, each self-contained within
  one contract's own window. Right for strategies that intentionally want gaps
  (e.g., only trade the last few days before each expiry, then sit out).
  - Roll Forward on: continuous exposure, always holding something, rolling
  forward every cycle — matches how a real "always short a monthly put" or
  "continuous carry" strategy actually trades, and is what Positional's own
  "Re-entry Rollover" does for the whole strategy — Roll Forward just gives you
  that same mechanic per-leg instead of strategy-wide.

● Good catch — that's happening because your Entry Time equals Exit Time (both
  15:15). That specific setup triggers a different mechanism I built earlier
  (per your own request: "it was built to re-enter same day") — which produces
  the same continuous chain even without Roll Forward. Let me verify exactly how
  similar/different the two modes actually are with your exact config, rather
  than assume.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Confirmed — zero difference between the two modes with your exact config
  (Entry Time = Exit Time = 15:15). My earlier example was wrong for your
  specific setup; let me give you the honest, verified picture.

  The reason: when Entry Time equals Exit Time, every cycle is "degenerate"
  (same day, same minute) — and the auto-reentry fix I built earlier (per your
  request: "it was built to re-enter same day") kicks in regardless of Roll 
  Forward, rolling straight into the next contract. So with matching entry/exit
  times, Roll Forward genuinely makes no difference — you'd get the identical
  tradesheet either way.

  The real difference only shows up when Entry Time and Exit Time are different.
  Let me verify that too, rather than just assert it.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Good — now I have the honest, correct contrast (my earlier example was wrong
  for your matching-times config, but this is the real difference, verified):

  Without Roll Forward (Entry 09:20, Exit 15:15 — different times)

  2024-01-24 09:20 → 2024-01-24 15:15   (SAME day, strike 21700)
  2024-02-28 09:20 → 2024-02-28 15:15   (SAME day, strike 22700)
  2024-03-27 09:20 → 2024-03-27 15:15   (SAME day, strike 22300)

  Every cycle enters and exits on the same day — a real, few-hours-long trade,
  then flat for the rest of the month until the next cycle's entry a month
  later. You're only in the market ~6 hours out of every ~30 days.

  With Roll Forward (same Entry/Exit times)

  2024-01-24 09:20 → 2024-02-28 15:15   (holds a full month, strike 21900)
  2024-02-28 15:15 → 2024-03-27 15:15   (continues immediately, strike 22700)
  2024-03-27 15:15 → 2024-04-24 15:15   (continues immediately, strike 22500)

  Each row holds for the entire month, and the next row's entry is literally the
  same moment the previous row exited — genuinely continuous exposure, never
  flat.

  So to directly answer your last message

  Your tradesheet showing continuous ROLLOVER chains without Roll Forward isn't
  Roll Forward secretly working — it's because your Entry Time and Exit Time are
  both set to 15:15. That specific combination triggers a separate mechanism
  (the same-day auto-reentry we built earlier) that happens to produce an
  identical continuous chain. If you ever set Entry Time and Exit Time to
  different values, that's when Roll Forward starts actually mattering — without
  it, you'd fall back to short same-day brackets with monthly gaps, like the
  first example above.

❯ so why are we having this toggle                                              

● Fair question — let me verify precisely where the toggle actually matters,
  rather than just assert it, since "same clock time" turned out to be an
  incidental trigger, not a deliberate design for continuity.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Confirmed — even with matching clock times (both 15:15), if Entry T-n ≠ Exit
  T-n, plain mode gives short brackets with monthly gaps, not continuity:

  2024-01-23 15:15 → 2024-01-24 15:15   (2-day bracket, then flat ~a month)
  2024-02-27 15:15 → 2024-02-28 15:15   (2-day bracket, then flat ~a month)

  Why the toggle exists

  "Same clock time" only produces continuity by a side effect: the auto-reentry
  fix I built earlier only fires when a cycle is exactly degenerate — same day
  and same minute. That requires Entry T-n = Exit T-n and matching clock times,
  simultaneously. Change either one, and continuity disappears, because there's
  no genuine "always roll forward" logic in plain mode — just a narrow patch for
  one specific coincidental configuration.

  So the toggle is what actually, deliberately, reliably says "hold this leg
  continuously, roll every cycle" — independent of whatever Entry/Exit times or
  T-n values you pick. Without it:

  - Different Entry/Exit clock times (the normal case — enter in the morning,
  exit near close) → no continuity at all, ever, in plain mode.
  - Different Entry/Exit T-n (e.g., "enter 2 days before expiry, exit 1 day
  before") → same problem, monthly gaps.
  - Continuity only ever "just happens" in the one narrow case where everything
  coincidentally matches — which isn't something you should have to
  reverse-engineer to get the behavior you actually want.

  Roll Forward is the explicit, first-class way to ask for continuous holding —
  matching how Positional's own "Re-entry Rollover" is its own separate checkbox
  too, not something implied by picking matching times.

(async function(){
  const sleep = ms =&gt; new Promise(r =&gt; setTimeout(r, ms));
  const setInput = (el, val) =&gt; {
    const setter =
Object.getOwnPropertyDescriptor(window.HTMLInputElement.prototype, 'value').set;
    setter.call(el, val);
    el.dispatchEvent(new Event('input', {bubbles:true}));
    el.dispatchEvent(new Event('change', {bubbles:true}));
  };
  const setSelect = (el, text) =&gt; {
    const setter =
Object.getOwnPropertyDescriptor(window.HTMLSelectElement.prototype,
'value').set;
    const opt = Array.from(el.options).find(o =&gt; o.text.trim() === text);
    if (!opt) return false;
    setter.call(el, opt.value);
    el.dispatchEvent(new Event('change', {bubbles:true}));
    return true;
  };
  const btnAll = t =&gt;
Array.from(document.querySelectorAll('button')).filter(b =&gt;
b.textContent.trim() === t);

  btnAll('Leg-Wise Roll')[0].click();

  await sleep(100);

setInput(t, '15:15'));
  await sleep(100);

  await sleep(100);

container

❯ whats the use of roll forwad if we remove it will it work ?

  if it's actually redundant here.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Confirmed — with Roll Forward OFF, identical config, the tradesheet came back
  byte-for-byte the same (same entries, exits, strikes, P&L, ROLLOVER reason).

  Why: in your current setup Entry T-n = Exit T-n = 1 and Entry Time = Exit Time
  = 15:15, so plain per-leg mode's "same-minute" auto-reentry (the fix built
  earlier) kicks in on every single cycle and independently walks forward one
  contract at a time — landing on exactly the same trade sequence Roll Forward
  produces on purpose.

  Roll Forward stops being redundant the moment either:
  - Entry T-n ≠ Exit T-n (e.g. enter T-1, exit T-2) — plain mode then resolves
  entry and exit against the same expiry with different offsets, so it never
  hits the degenerate case and just runs each cycle independently instead of
  chaining contracts.
  - Entry Time ≠ Exit Time — same reason, no degeneracy trigger, no
  auto-continuation.

  So for your exact current parameters, removing it changes nothing. If you ever
  set different T-n's or different entry/exit clock times, Roll Forward is what
  still gives you the continuous "hold the next contract from day one" chain
  instead of falling back to plain non-overlapping per-cycle trades.

✻ Churned for 1m 2s

※ recap: You're building leg-wise roll forward for the intraday backtester. I just confirmed live that with your current settings (T-n and times matching on entry/exit), toggling Roll Forward on or off gives identical results, so no action needed unless you want to test differing T-n/times.

● Here's the complete setup checklist for the other PC — the zip alone covers
  code + data but not the runtime deps or the systemd wiring:

  1. Prerequisites (install on the new PC)

  # Rust 1.96.0 (matches rust-toolchain.toml, will auto-select once you cd in)
  curl --proto '=https' --tlsv1.2 -sSf https://sh.rustup.rs | sh
  # Node 22.x for the frontend

  2. Unzip to the right place

  The zip stores absolute paths as home/aff34/Downloads/intraday-algo/... and
  data/intraday/.... If the new PC's username isn't aff34, unzip to a temp spot
  and move the pieces, or just recreate the same user/paths:
  sudo unzip "intraday-algo-full.zip" -d /
  # → lands at /home/aff34/Downloads/intraday-algo and /data/intraday

  3. Build

  cd /home/aff34/Downloads/intraday-algo
  cargo build --release          # target/ wasn't in the zip, this rebuilds it
  cd frontend && npm install && npm run build   # node_modules is in the zip but
  rebuild is safer cross-machine

  4. Run it as a service (what makes it "iserver.service" here)

  This part is not in the zip — it's a local systemd user unit at
  ~/.config/systemd/user/iserver.service:
  [Unit]
  Description=intraday-algo iserver

  [Service]
  WorkingDirectory=/home/aff34/Downloads/intraday-algo
  Environment=INTRADAY_DATA_DIR=/data/intraday INTRADAY_GATE_MB=3000
  INTRADAY_PORT=8200
  ExecStart=/home/aff34/Downloads/intraday-algo/target/release/iserver
  Restart=always
  RestartSec=2

  [Install]
  WantedBy=default.target
  Save that on the new PC, then:
  systemctl --user daemon-reload
  systemctl --user enable --now iserver.service
  Or skip systemd entirely and just run ./start.sh (it builds + launches API on
  8200 and a UI dev server on 4200 directly, no service needed).

  5. Verify

  curl localhost:8200/health

  Note: /data/intraday is ~13GB total (mostly NIFTY/ at 12GB) — make sure the
  new PC has that much free plus room for the target/ build (~7GB). Ports
  8200/4200 must be free (won't collide with the separate EOD app, which uses
  8000/3000).
